Semisimplicity means that the representation splits into irreducible pieces; continuity respects the Galois and \(\ell\)-adic topologies.

4.2 Compare information that does not depend on a basis.

A change of basis replaces a matrix \(A\) by \(BAB^{-1}\). Its entries change, but its characteristic polynomial and trace do not:

\[ P_A(T)=\det(TI-A),\qquad P_{BAB^{-1}}(T)=P_A(T),\qquad \operatorname{tr}(BAB^{-1})=\operatorname{tr}(A). \]

These invariants let arithmetic and automorphic constructions be compared without choosing the same basis. Frobenius is the local arithmetic operator whose eigenvalues enter familiar unramified comparisons. At ramified places, a Weil–Deligne parameter records both a Weil-group representation and a nilpotent monodromy operator \(N\). This section’s polynomial formula explains the language of the comparison; it does not assert unrestricted Frobenius compatibility. The actual local conclusions are stated below.

4.3 Retain the infinity type and its conjugation symmetry.

The theorem also supplies an integer \(\mathrm{wt}\) and integer multisets \(\mathrm{HT}(\tau)\), one for each embedding \(\tau:E\to\overline{\mathbb Q}_\ell\). If \(Z_{\iota\tau}\) denotes the automorphic infinitesimal-character multiset, the source fixes the normalization by

\[ \{h:h\in\mathrm{HT}(\tau)\} =\left\{\frac{a-1}{2}-z:z\in Z_{\iota\tau}\right\}. \]

This is a multiset equality, retaining multiplicities and viewing the integers in \(\mathbb C\). Complex conjugation \(c_E\) on the CM field pairs the weights:

\[ \mathrm{HT}(\tau\circ c_E) =\{\mathrm{wt}-h:h\in\mathrm{HT}(\tau)\}. \]

Thus a weight on one embedding determines its partner on the conjugate embedding. Above \(\ell\), the theorem identifies these multisets with the appropriate jumps under its comparison hypotheses. In the spherical local case it supplies crystalline and de Rham properties; the separate “nice” case supplies de Rham, potentially semistable, jump, and local-parameter conclusions under additional conditions. It does not declare every local component crystalline.

4.4 Follow the construction from geometry to the final representation.

The construction has two different tasks. First, geometric cohomology supplies representations for specially arranged automorphic forms. Then congruences and trace limits remove those extra arrangements. Theta lifting changes the rank, and descent removes auxiliary field conditions. Keeping these tasks separate explains why neither a geometric representation of the wrong dimension nor a collection of matching traces is already the final theorem.

4.4.1 Extract the automorphic information from geometric cohomology.

The geometric input contains a tower with Hecke correspondences, a chosen system of Hecke eigenvalues, and an algebraic character used to normalize the result. The cohomology of the tower carries a Galois action. Correspondences defined over the ground field commute with that action, so selecting their eigendata leaves a Galois-stable piece. In the familiar generalized-eigenspace notation, the mechanism is

\[ V_\theta=\bigcap_T\ker(T-\theta(T))^{m_T}, \qquad \rho(g)T=T\rho(g) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \rho(g)V_\theta\subseteq V_\theta. \]

This formula explains the selection mechanism; the source implements it through its geometric tower and matrix-realization conditions. Passing to the semisimplification retains traces and characteristic polynomials. The first output can have dimension \(d\) larger than the desired automorphic rank \(a\). Further extraction is therefore required.

At a suitable place away from \(\ell\), the geometric Hecke–Frobenius relation gives an annihilating polynomial. Schematically,

\[ Q_w(F_w)=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \text{every eigenvalue of }F_w\text{ is a root of }Q_w. \]

An annihilating polynomial controls possible eigenvalues; it does not by itself give their multiplicities or identify the characteristic polynomial. The geometric package also records Hodge occurrences and crystalline comparisons at the specified coefficient-prime places. These additional data are used when extracting the rank-\(a\) seed.

4.4.2 Remove the extra character and arrange usable seeds.

The geometric eigendata include an algebraic Hecke character \(\kappa\). If the geometric polynomial has roots \(\alpha_j\kappa(w)^{-1}\), twisting by the corresponding Galois character restores the intended roots:

\[ \{\alpha_j\kappa(w)^{-1}\}_j \xrightarrow{\ \otimes\kappa_\ell\ } \{\alpha_j\}_j. \]

Weights must be corrected at the same time: tensoring by a character adds its Hodge–Tate weight to every weight of the representation. Correcting the eigenvalues while leaving the weights unchanged would give the wrong normalization.

The first seed theorem works in odd rank with auxiliary field conditions, two selected split places above \(\ell\), specified local automorphic type, separated weights, and genericity conditions at finitely many chosen places. Its output is a continuous semisimple rank-\(a\) representation. It has a dense-set Frobenius comparison and stronger local conclusions at the finite set being controlled. These hypotheses make the geometric extraction possible; the target automorphic representation need not already satisfy all of them.

The quantifier order matters. For every finite collection \(Y\) of places we want to inspect, the construction may choose a suitable seed. The eventual representation must work for all places, so the next steps coordinate these separate choices.

4.4.3 Approximate entire eigenvalue systems, including their relations.

Write \(\theta=(\theta_j)_{j\in J}\) for the target Hecke eigenvalues and \(\eta_i=(\eta_{i,j})_{j\in J}\) for systems carried by usable seeds \(\rho_i\). The congruence theorem supplies more than approximation of each coordinate separately. For every \(\epsilon>0\), it supplies a finite set \(I_\epsilon\) and coefficients \(c_i\) such that, simultaneously for every integral polynomial in finitely many coordinates,

\[ \left|P(\theta)-\sum_{i\in I_\epsilon}c_iP(\eta_i)\right|_\ell \le\epsilon, \qquad P\in\mathbb Z[X_j:j\in J]. \]

This is the source's polynomial approximation condition. The coefficients need not be positive, and the target need not be the limit of a single sequence of eigenforms. Taking \(P=X_j\) controls an individual eigenvalue. Taking a polynomial relation controls how different eigenvalues fit together. That second feature is essential for eventually recovering a representation.

The same approximation can include finitely many local power-sum coordinates above \(\ell\), while retaining fixed weights away from the two distinguished places. The congruence theorem also places the target eigenvalues in a finite extension \(L/\mathbb Q_\ell\). This supplies a complete coefficient field in which limits can be taken.

4.4.4 Extend the prescribed traces from dense arithmetic data.

Suitable Hecke operators give elements \(x_j\in G_E\). For each seed there is a set \(D_i\subseteq J\) on which

\[ \eta_{i,j}=\operatorname{tr}\rho_i(x_j) \quad(j\in D_i), \qquad \overline{\{x_j:j\in\bigcap_{i\in I_0}D_i\}}=G_E \quad\text{for every finite }I_0. \]

The finite-intersection clause lets one compare several seeds on a common dense set. Their traces are continuous. Consequently, a uniform estimate obtained on that set extends to every group element. The trace-limit theorem constructs \(t:G_E\to L\) with the same approximation bounds:

\[ \left|t(g)-\sum_{i\in I_\epsilon} c_i\operatorname{tr}\rho_i(g)\right|_\ell \le\epsilon \qquad(g\in G_E). \]

Where a prescribed coordinate is shared by all the relevant seeds, \(t(x_j)=\theta_j\). Completeness prevents the values from escaping the coefficient field; the uniform approximation by continuous traces gives continuity. Density alone would establish uniqueness of a continuous extension, but would not establish that an extension exists. The approximation estimates supply that missing existence argument.

4.4.5 Prove that the limiting function really is a trace.

A continuous function on a Galois group need not be the trace of a representation. Traces of \(a\)-dimensional representations satisfy polynomial identities. Two are

\[ t(1)=a,\qquad t(gh)=t(hg). \]

A further alternating identity encodes the dimension. One way to write the source's recursive form is

\[ \begin{aligned} A_0(t)&=1,\\ A_{m+1}(t;g_0,\ldots,g_m) &=t(g_0)A_m(t;g_1,\ldots,g_m)\\ &\quad-\sum_{j=1}^{m} A_m(t;g_1,\ldots,g_0g_j,\ldots,g_m),\\ A_{a+1}(t;g_0,\ldots,g_a)&=0. \end{aligned} \]

For example, when \(a=1\), the last relation is \(t(g)t(h)=t(gh)\): the trace is a character. In higher rank, it is the corresponding matrix trace identity.

Polynomial approximation preserves every such identity. If \(P\) vanishes on all seed traces, then

\[ |P(t)|_\ell =\left|P(t)-\sum_i c_iP(\operatorname{tr}\rho_i)\right|_\ell \le\epsilon \quad\text{for every }\epsilon>0, \qquad P(t)=0. \]

The function \(t\) is therefore a dimension-\(a\) pseudocharacter, meaning that it satisfies the trace identities. The characteristic-zero realization theorem then supplies a semisimple representation \(r\) with \(\operatorname{tr}r=t\). A separate continuity theorem for semisimple representations upgrades the continuity of this trace to continuity of \(r\). These are genuine steps: taking a linear combination of representation matrices would not preserve the multiplication law.

\[ \text{polynomially approximated traces} \Longrightarrow t\text{ is a pseudocharacter} \Longrightarrow r:G_E\to\mathrm{GL}_a(\overline{\mathbb Q}_\ell), \quad r(gh)=r(g)r(h). \]

4.4.6 Carry local information through the limit.

Recovering a global representation does not automatically preserve crystalline behavior or monodromy. The source therefore chooses the approximating seeds separately for each finite set of places. At a place above \(\ell\), it keeps the relevant Hodge jumps fixed and approximates local Frobenius power sums jointly with the global traces. The closedness and comparison results then transfer the specified local conclusions.

Power sums explain why this carries polynomial information. If \(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_a\) are eigenvalues, put \(p_j=\sum_i\alpha_i^j\). Newton's identities recover the characteristic-polynomial coefficients \(e_k\):

\[ e_0=1,\qquad ke_k=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j-1}e_{k-j}p_j, \qquad \det(T-A)=\sum_{k=0}^{a}(-1)^ke_kT^{a-k}. \]

Thus matching the appropriate power sums recovers the polynomial in characteristic zero. It still does not recover the monodromy operator by itself. That is why the proof retains its crystalline, fixed-weight, and local-parameter hypotheses rather than treating trace convergence as a universal local compatibility theorem.

Different choices of finite test set must yield the same global semisimple representation up to isomorphism: the prescribed traces agree on dense arithmetic data. This uniqueness allows a local conclusion proved using one convenient family of seeds to be attached to the common representation.

4.4.7 Reach other ranks by lifting, then removing known characters.

The geometric seed argument starts in odd rank. For an even target rank, the theta input supplies lifts to sufficiently large odd rank, with compatible characters and weights. The relevant weight inequality has the form

\[ b+m_W(v)\le2\lambda_{v,k} \qquad\text{for every archimedean }v\text{ and weight index }k, \]

where \(b\) is the target rank of the lift. Auxiliary characters are chosen along with \(b\) so that this inequality and the required restrictions of those characters hold. Merely choosing a large rank would not suffice.

The lift adds explicit character contributions to the Satake data. The source uses two lifts whose extra character families are disjoint. After matching characteristic polynomials on dense arithmetic data, the resulting representations satisfy an identity of the form

\[ P_{R_1(g)}(T)\prod_j(T-\chi_{2,j}(g)) =P_{R_2(g)}(T)\prod_i(T-\chi_{1,i}(g)), \qquad \chi_{1,i}\ne\chi_{2,j}. \]

Semisimplicity turns this identity into a statement about actual constituents. A character occurring on one side must occur on the other; disjointness prevents it from being absorbed by the other auxiliary family. It must therefore occur inside the corresponding \(R_i\), where it can be split off. Repeating this removes the known lines and leaves the desired lower-rank representation.

\[ R_i\simeq r\oplus\bigoplus_j\chi_{i,j}, \qquad P_{R_i(g)}(T)=P_{r(g)}(T)\prod_j(T-\chi_{i,j}(g)). \]

These equations describe the character-removal step after the prescribed twists. The proof supplies the stable summands before cancelling their polynomial factors. It also transfers continuity, semisimplicity, Hodge jumps and the permitted local comparisons to the remaining summand.

4.4.8 Remove the auxiliary field conditions by compatible descent.

Auxiliary CM extensions arrange splitting and local conditions needed earlier. Constructing a representation over an extension \(E'/E\) gives an action of the smaller group \(G_{E'}\); extending it to \(G_E\) requires compatibility. The source combines automorphic base change, comparisons on overlaps, and representation patching. In schematic form the compatibility being sought is

\[ r_{E'}|_{G_{E'E''}}\simeq r_{E''}|_{G_{E'E''}}, \qquad r_E|_{G_{E'}}\simeq r_{E'}. \]

Matching semisimple traces identifies the overlap representations. The descent theorem handles the further consistency needed to obtain the global action. Local properties are then descended by separate results for unramifiedness, de Rham and crystalline comparisons, and local Frobenius data. They are not all consequences of the abstract existence of \(r_E\).

4.4.9 State what this chain has established, and where its inputs enter.

The resulting assembly has the following shape:

\[ \begin{gathered} \text{geometric eigenspaces and local comparisons} \Longrightarrow\text{odd-rank seeds},\\ \text{seeds + congruences} \Longrightarrow\text{continuous semisimple trace-limit representations},\\ \text{theta lifts + character removal + descent} \Longrightarrow\text{the required ranks and fields},\\ \text{local comparison theorems} \Longrightarrow\text{the precise conclusions in section 4.5}. \end{gathered} \]

At this assembly entry point, the geometric realization package and the theta-lift existence statement remain explicit hypotheses, named GeoOne and TLiftK in the source. Writing a proposition or packaging its conclusions is not a proof of it. The geometric package is itself assembled from the geometric tower, Hodge, correspondence and realization results; the theta package carries its own character and weight conditions. The steps above explain how those inputs are used and why the intermediate constructions work. They do not infer that the final short assembly theorem proves all of arithmetic geometry or theta lifting on its own.

Three useful checks: the passage from seed traces to a representation; pseudocharacter realization and continuity; and the final conditional assembly.

4.5 Keep the local conclusions at their stated strength.

At a place \(v\nmid\ell\), write \(X_v\) for the Weil–Deligne data from \(r\), and \(Y_v\) for the parameter of the algebraically normalized local automorphic representation. The comparison always gives

\[\dim X_v=\dim Y_v.\]

Under the source’s \(\mathrm{Tor}\) condition and \(N_{Y_v}=0\), it gives \(X_v=Y_v\). A different clause assumes the source’s tame and squarefree conditions, zero monodromy on \(Y_v\), a continuous field identification \(E_v\cong\mathbb Q_p\), and

\[ a=1\quad\text{or}\quad16a^2<p. \]

That clause concludes

\[ N_{X_v}=0,\qquad P_{X_v(g)}(T)=P_{Y_v(g)}(T) \quad\text{for every Weil element }g\text{ with }\deg g=0. \]

The degree-zero restriction matters: it is not a blanket equality for arbitrary Frobenius powers. These are the local comparisons available for subsequent arithmetic arguments.

Selective source links: representation, weight, and local conditions; geometry, congruences, trace limits, and descent.

From cohomology to integral deformation theory

5 Deformation rings and cotangent modules.

Represent locally trivial cohomology classes by constrained deformations, then lift their derivatives integrally to bound their length.

5.1 Specify the cohomology that the deformation argument measures.

A Galois representation does not, by its existence alone, bound a cohomology group. The source uses a particular character module, embeds its classes into a three-dimensional deformation problem, and then controls that problem using automorphy and commutative algebra. This expansion follows the generic odd-prime branch of that argument. The exceptional-prime estimates and the later selection of arithmetic windows remain separate steps.

  1. 5.2Make each cohomology class visible
  2. 5.3Turn cocycles into deformations
  3. 5.4Put the deformations into one ring
  4. 5.5Identify the cotangent quotient
  5. 5.6Bound all torsion layers
  6. 7Use automorphy and rigidity
  7. 8.1Measure congruences by traces
  8. 8.3Sum over coefficient primes

Let \(\ell\) be the coefficient prime, let \(\mathcal O\) be the ring of integers of a finite extension \(E_\ell/\mathbb Q_\ell\), and write \(\varpi\) for a uniformizer. Fix a positive twist \(r\) and a finite-order character \(\psi\) of level \(N\), viewed as a Galois character with values in \(\mathcal O^\times\). Put

\[ \Psi=\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{\,r}\psi,\qquad D=E_\ell/\mathcal O,\qquad g\cdot t=\Psi(g)^{-1}t\quad\text{on }D(\Psi^{-1}). \]

The relevant group, denoted here by \(\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}\), consists of classes that are trivial on decomposition groups at primes dividing \(\ell N\), and trivial on inertia at every finite prime. With \(G_q\) and \(I_q\) denoting those local subgroups, its defining restriction map is

\[ \mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi} =\ker\!\left[ H^1(G_{\mathbb Q},D(\Psi^{-1})) \longrightarrow \prod_{q\mid\ell N}H^1(G_q,D(\Psi^{-1})) \times\prod_q H^1(I_q,D(\Psi^{-1})) \right]. \]

Restriction to inertia being zero means unramified as a cohomology class; vanishing on a decomposition group is stronger. These are character-cohomology groups with specified local conditions. The earlier field-cohomology problem reaches them through character decomposition and comparison results; it is not being identified with the full adjoint cohomology of an arbitrary automorphic representation.

Which hypotheses delimit this branch?

The source fixes an auxiliary imaginary quadratic field \(K\), takes \(N\) even and \(r\ge1\), and requires \(\ell\ne2\), \(\ell\nmid\operatorname{ord}(\psi)\), \(\psi(-1)=(-1)^r\), \(\ell\nmid|\operatorname{disc}K|h_K\), and \(\gcd(|\operatorname{disc}K|,N)=1\). Its good-data condition additionally imposes a square-one condition on a derived character, a residual independence condition involving \(K\), and \(\ell-1\nmid2r\). Here \(h_K\) is the class number. These restrictions enable the markings, local comparisons and automorphy statements used below. The displayed bound is asserted only when that full condition holds; the separate exceptional branches handle other cases.

5.2 Choose a faithful representative of each torsion class.

Write \(\alpha=\Psi^{-1}\). A continuous cocycle \(z:G_{\mathbb Q}\to D\) satisfies

\[ z(gh)=z(g)+\alpha(g)z(h). \]

Two cocycles represent the same class when their difference is \((\alpha(g)-1)t\) for a fixed \(t\in D\). Thus simply counting cocycles would count changes of representative as new arithmetic information. A marking chooses \(g_1\) with \(\alpha(g_1)-1\) a unit and replaces \(z\) by

\[ \widetilde z(g)=z(g) -(\alpha(g)-1)(\alpha(g_1)-1)^{-1}z(g_1), \qquad \widetilde z(g_1)=0. \]

This normalization is independent of the representative: adding a coboundary changes both terms by the same amount. A normalized coboundary is zero, because evaluating it at \(g_1\) forces its defining \(t\) to vanish. Consequently distinct classes give distinct normalized cocycles.

Now take a class killed by a nonzero \(b\in\mathcal O\). Normalization commutes with multiplication by \(b\), so \(b\widetilde z=0\). Its values therefore lie in a finite coefficient module:

\[ \mathcal O/b\mathcal O\simeq D[b]=b^{-1}\mathcal O/\mathcal O, \qquad t\bmod b\longmapsto t/b\bmod\mathcal O. \]

On \(G_K\), the split reference representation is \(\rho_0=\operatorname{diag}(\Psi,1,\Psi^{-1})\). Embed a scalar value into a matrix by

\[ M(t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\t&0&0\\0&-t&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad \rho_0(g)M(t)\rho_0(g)^{-1}=M(\Psi(g)^{-1}t). \]

The two off-diagonal positions transform by exactly the character acting on our coefficients. The minus sign makes this line compatible with the conjugate-self-dual extension of the construction from \(G_K\) to \(G_{\mathbb Q}\). The source consequently injects \(\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}[b]\) into a specified collection of normalized matrix cocycles. Their local vanishing and local coboundary conditions are retained. This explicit injection is the reason this particular cohomology group can enter the deformation problem.

5.3 Read the cocycle equation as a first-order representation law.

Introduce a formal infinitesimal \(\epsilon\) with \(\epsilon^2=0\). For matrix coefficients \(c(g)\), consider

\[ \rho_\epsilon(g)=(1+\epsilon c(g))\rho_0(g). \]

On \(G_K\), multiplying two such matrices gives

\[ \rho_\epsilon(g)\rho_\epsilon(h) =\bigl[1+\epsilon\{c(g)+\rho_0(g)c(h)\rho_0(g)^{-1}\}\bigr]\rho_0(gh). \]

The representation law holds precisely when \(c(gh)=c(g)+\operatorname{Ad}(\rho_0(g))c(h)\): the matrix cocycle equation. Conjugating by \(1+\epsilon X\) replaces \(c(g)\) by \(c(g)+X-\operatorname{Ad}(\rho_0(g))X\), a coboundary change. This explains the familiar link between first cohomology and infinitesimal deformations.

For torsion coefficients the source uses the square-zero algebra \(\mathcal O\oplus\epsilon(\mathcal O/b\mathcal O)\), whose multiplication is

\[ (a,\epsilon u)(a',\epsilon u') =(aa',\epsilon(au'+a'u)). \]

That retains the coefficient ring and the depth of the torsion. It also retains the conjugate-self-dual structure and the selected local conditions. The unrestricted slogan “the tangent space is \(H^1\)” would lose those restrictions and would not by itself give the bound we need.

5.4 Encode all admissible deformations in one ring.

A deformation ring \(R\) organizes lifts of the fixed residual representation into complete local coefficient algebras. After the source's basis normalizations and local conditions are fixed, its universal property has the form

\[ \{\text{admissible normalized lifts to }B\} \simeq\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{\mathcal O,\mathrm{loc}}(R,B), \qquad \rho_B=f_*\rho_R. \]

The matrix entries of \(\rho_R\) serve as coordinates. Relations express the group law, the conjugate-self-dual symmetry, ramification restrictions and the chosen local deformation condition above \(\ell\). The marking removes specified changes of basis. Thus a cohomology class now gives a map from one fixed ring to a square-zero algebra, instead of a separately constructed representation.

The split representation determines a base map \(x_0:R\to\mathcal O\). A map reducing to \(x_0\) has the form \(f(a)=x_0(a)+\epsilon\delta(a)\). Multiplicativity is equivalent to the derivation rule

\[ \delta(aa')=x_0(a)\delta(a')+x_0(a')\delta(a), \qquad \delta|_{\mathcal O}=0. \]

This turns the deformation question into linear algebra on a module of differentials at the base point. No identification of \(R\) with a Hecke algebra is asserted in this passage; the source uses a more specific graded model and comparison theorem.

5.5 Identify the exact cotangent quotient being measured.

First recall the elementary construction. If \(J=\ker x_0\), a derivation vanishes on \(J^2\), since both factors have zero base value. Hence

\[ \operatorname{Der}_{\mathcal O,x_0}(R,Q) \simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal O}(J/J^2,Q). \]

The quotient \(J/J^2\) is the cotangent module: it keeps linear changes and discards their products. The source refines this construction. It presents the constrained ring through a graded model algebra \(A\), a base map \(x:A\to\mathcal O\), and homogeneous equations. Diagonal conjugation gives weights \(-2,-1,0,1,2\) on matrix entries; the cocycles from §5.2 occupy weight \(-1\).

Set \(J_A=\ker x\). The source first saturates the square with respect to nonzero coefficients:

\[ J_A^{2,\mathrm{sat}} =\{a\in A:\exists d\in\mathcal O\setminus\{0\},\ da\in J_A^2\}, \qquad \Lambda_{-1}=\operatorname{im}\bigl(A_{-1}\cap J_A\longrightarrow A/J_A^{2,\mathrm{sat}}\bigr). \]

This forms a lattice of linear directions, with coefficient torsion removed. Let \(F_1,\ldots,F_m\) be the weight \(-1\) equations in the model, and let \(\overline F_i\) be their linear images in \(\Lambda_{-1}\). The module that enters the estimate is

\[ C_{-1}=\Lambda_{-1}/\langle\overline F_1,\ldots,\overline F_m\rangle_{\mathcal O}. \]

Its torsion measures how far the integral equations are from spanning the full lattice of relevant directions. Saturating the square did not discard this information: the torsion here comes from the index of the equation lattice. It is this selected quotient, rather than the whole ordinary cotangent module, whose length controls the character cohomology.

5.6 Measure the depth of every torsion layer.

A tangent-space dimension records how many directions remain modulo \(\varpi\). It does not record how long they survive. For example, \(\mathcal O/\varpi\mathcal O\) and \(\mathcal O/\varpi^{100}\mathcal O\) both have a one-dimensional quotient modulo \(\varpi\), but their lengths are \(1\) and \(100\).

The delicate step is to turn a torsion cocycle into a functional on the saturated cotangent lattice. We keep the good-data hypotheses from §5.1, the marked normalization, and the source's local deformation condition. In particular, \(\mathcal O\) is a complete discrete valuation ring with finite residue field, \(2\) is invertible, and the chosen model has the relative presentation described below. These hypotheses supply an integral lift on the ambient model; the universal deformation property alone does not supply it.

  1. 5.6.1Identify the length to bound
  2. 5.6.2Use the local splitting witness
  3. 5.6.3Lift the splitting matrix
  4. 5.6.4Preserve the local equations
  5. 5.6.5Match the global derivative
  6. 5.6.6Use the relative presentation
  7. 5.6.7Lift the linear coefficients
  8. 5.6.8Pass through saturation
  9. 5.6.9Recover the original cocycle
  10. 5.6.10Count every torsion layer

5.6.1 Identify the integral quantity that must bound the cocycles.

Under the model's spanning hypotheses, elementary divisors put its equation lattice in diagonal form. After changing generators, with \(d\) the rank of \(\Lambda_{-1}\), its nonzero linear generators have the following form; any redundant linear generators can be taken to be zero:

\[ \overline F_i=\varpi^{a_i}y_i,\qquad C_{-1}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal O/\varpi^{a_i}\mathcal O, \qquad \operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}C_{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_i. \]

Fix a nonzero \(b\in\mathcal O\) and put \(Q=\mathcal O/b\mathcal O\). We will attach a distinct element of \(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal O}(C_{-1},Q)\) to each class in \(\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}[b]\). The bound will then follow by counting the possible images of the generators \(y_i\).

5.6.2 Use the local splitting witness, not just the global cocycle equation.

Let \(\gamma\) be the normalized matrix cocycle attached to a class. Write \(H\to G_{\mathbb Q}\) for the chosen local decomposition-group map above \(\ell\), and write \(h\star X\) for the polarized adjoint action of the split reference representation. The local condition supplies a matrix \(X\) such that

\[ \gamma(h)=X-h\star X,\qquad X=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\\bar u&0&0\\0&\bar v&0\end{pmatrix} \in M_3(Q). \]

Here and below \(\gamma(h)\) means restriction along that local map. The two allowed positions have weight \(-1\). Local triviality therefore provides more than a vanishing statement in cohomology: it provides a concrete change of basis that splits the local deformation. Lifting this matrix is easier than lifting an arbitrary global cohomology class.

To keep track of the polarization, use indices \(0,1,2\), put \((w_0,w_1,w_2)=(1,0,-1)\), and let \(\eta\) be the quadratic character of \(K/\mathbb Q\). On the reference representation the action is

\[ (h\star Z)_{ij}=\Psi(h)^{w_i-w_j} \begin{cases} Z_{ij},&\eta(h)=1,\\ -Z_{2-j,\,2-i},&\eta(h)=-1. \end{cases} \]

The coefficient character is reduced to \(Q^\times\) when \(Z\) has coefficients in \(Q\). The reflected indices have the same weight as the original entry, so the weight \(-1\) subspace is preserved.

5.6.3 Lift the splitting matrix while preserving the local continuity condition.

Choose \(u,v\in\mathcal O\) lifting \(\bar u,\bar v\), and form

\[ Y=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\u&0&0\\0&v&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad Y\bmod b=X, \qquad (Y-h\star Y)\bmod b=\gamma(h). \]

The source also checks the continuity needed for this integral coboundary. If \(\bar v=-\bar u\), choose \(v=-u\). Then \(Y_{ij}=-Y_{2-j,2-i}\), and the sign-and-reflection part of the action is independent of which value \(\eta(h)\) takes. Only the already continuous character \(\Psi\) varies.

If \(\bar v\ne-\bar u\), the two sign cases can be distinguished from the original locally constant cocycle. Indeed, its \((2,1)\) entry gives

\[ \overline{\Psi(h)}\bigl(\bar v-\gamma(h)_{21}\bigr) =\begin{cases} \bar v,&\eta(h)=1,\\ -\bar u,&\eta(h)=-1. \end{cases} \]

The left side is locally constant: \(\gamma\) is locally constant and the continuous character \(\Psi\) becomes locally constant modulo \(b\). Since the two values on the right are distinct, the branch is locally constant too. Replacing those two values by the corresponding entries of \(Y\) preserves this property. Thus either choice of case yields an admissible continuous integral coboundary.

5.6.4 Keep the integral local deformation inside its defining ideal.

Work over \(\mathcal O[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^2)\). The matrix \(1+\epsilon Y\) is invertible, with inverse \(1-\epsilon Y\). Conjugating the reference representation gives

\[ \rho_{\mathrm{loc},\epsilon}(h) =(1+\epsilon Y)\rho_0(h)(1-\epsilon Y) =\bigl(1+\epsilon(Y-h\star Y)\bigr)\rho_0(h). \]

This formula uses the polarized group law when \(\eta(h)=-1\). It automatically satisfies the representation law because it is a conjugate of a representation.

Let \(R_{\mathrm{loc}}\) represent the local lifts and let \(I_{\mathrm{loc}}\) impose the chosen local condition. The source's local comparison supplies a base map \(x_{\mathrm{loc}}\) killing \(I_{\mathrm{loc}}\), together with stability of that condition under changes of basis congruent to the identity. Since \(1+\epsilon Y\) is such a change, its classifying map satisfies

\[ F_{\mathrm{loc}}:R_{\mathrm{loc}}\longrightarrow \mathcal O[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^2),\qquad F_{\mathrm{loc}}(a)=x_{\mathrm{loc}}(a)+\epsilon D_{\mathrm{loc}}(a), \qquad F_{\mathrm{loc}}(I_{\mathrm{loc}})=0. \]

Comparing the two coefficients shows that \(D_{\mathrm{loc}}\) is an integral derivation and \(D_{\mathrm{loc}}(I_{\mathrm{loc}})=0\). It has weight \(-1\), because the coboundary of \(Y\) has only those matrix positions. This is where the specified local deformation condition is used: stability under conjugation keeps its equations valid over the integral dual numbers.

5.6.5 Match the integral local derivative to the global derivative modulo \(b\).

The global cocycle defines a representation over \(\mathcal O\oplus\epsilon Q\). Its normalization, its vanishing on the designated ramification subgroups, and the reduction of \(F_{\mathrm{loc}}\) verify the conditions defining the global ring \(R\). The universal property then gives

\[ F:R\longrightarrow\mathcal O\oplus\epsilon Q, \qquad F(a)=x_0(a)+\epsilon D_R(a). \]

For a matrix-entry coordinate \(r_{ij}(g)\) of the universal representation, comparison with \((1+\epsilon\gamma(g))\rho_0(g)\) gives the explicit formula

\[ D_R(r_{ij}(g))=\overline{\Psi(g)}^{\,w_j}\gamma(g)_{ij}. \]

The exponent comes from the \(j\)-th diagonal entry of \(\rho_0(g)\), multiplying the \(j\)-th column. Restricting this formula to \(H\) agrees with \(D_{\mathrm{loc}}\bmod b\), since the two local cocycles agree. Derivations on the represented deformation ring are determined by these matrix-entry coordinates, so the local and global derivatives are compatible on the entire local ring.

Let \(q_A:A\to R\) be the model map and set \(\delta=D_R\circ q_A\). We have now constructed the required derivative modulo \(b\). The next step lifts \(\delta\) to \(\mathcal O\) using this local compatibility.

5.6.6 Separate the local relations from the additional global coordinates.

The model construction provides a weighted presentation with local variables \(\mathbf u\) and additional variables \(\mathbf z\). Write its form as

\[ B_{\mathrm{loc}}=R_{\mathrm{loc}}/I_{\mathrm{loc}} \simeq\mathcal O[[\mathbf u]]/\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{loc}}, \qquad \mathcal C\simeq \frac{\mathcal O[[\mathbf u,\mathbf z]]} {\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal O[[\mathbf u,\mathbf z]]}. \]

All variables have zero base value. The graded algebra \(A\) sits inside this ambient algebra \(\mathcal C\), and the map \(\mathcal C\to R\) restricts to \(q_A\). The significant property is the displayed equality of relation ideals: the ambient relations are generated by the local relations. The further equations defining the global problem are imposed by the map to \(R\). This relative presentation is a supplied part of the model construction, justified there using its local presentation and relation-count results.

Consequently we can prescribe an integral derivative on the local variables using \(D_{\mathrm{loc}}\), and lift the derivative values on the new variables individually. Put

\[ d(u_i)=D_{\mathrm{loc}}(u_i),\qquad d(z_j)= \begin{cases} \text{any lift of }D_R(q_{\mathcal C}(z_j)), &\deg z_j=-1,\\ 0,&\deg z_j\ne-1. \end{cases} \]

Here \(q_{\mathcal C}:\mathcal C\to R\) denotes the ambient map, and variables also denote their quotient images. The old values already reduce correctly by the local compatibility. The zero choices reduce correctly because \(D_R\) vanishes on coordinates of other weights. The values \(D_{\mathrm{loc}}(u_i)\) also vanish unless \(\deg u_i=-1\).

5.6.7 Build the integral derivative and check every ambient relation.

For a power series \(P\), let \([u_i]P\) and \([z_j]P\) denote its linear coefficients. Define

\[ L(P)=\sum_i [u_i]P\,d(u_i)+\sum_j[z_j]P\,d(z_j). \]

There are finitely many variables, so this is a finite sum. The coefficient identity

\[ [v](PQ)=P(0)[v]Q+Q(0)[v]P \quad\Longrightarrow\quad L(PQ)=P(0)L(Q)+Q(0)L(P) \]

proves the derivation rule. On series in the old variables, \(L\) agrees with \(D_{\mathrm{loc}}\) after passing to \(B_{\mathrm{loc}}\): both derivatives have the prescribed values on all \(u_i\). Thus \(L(f)=0\) for every \(f\in\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{loc}}\). Also \(f(0)=0\), since the origin is defined on the quotient. For an arbitrary series \(H\),

\[ L(Hf)=H(0)L(f)+f(0)L(H)=0. \]

Additivity now shows that \(L\) kills the entire extended relation ideal. It descends to \(\mathcal C\); restricting to \(A\) gives an integral derivation \(\widetilde\delta\). A homogeneous element of weight other than \(-1\) has no linear term in a variable of weight \(-1\), while all other assigned derivative values are zero. Therefore

\[ \widetilde\delta:A\longrightarrow\mathcal O, \qquad \widetilde\delta(A_j)=0\quad(j\ne-1), \qquad \widetilde\delta\bmod b=\delta. \]

The last equality follows by agreement on the variables: a series differs from its constant and linear terms by an element of the variable ideal squared, which an origin derivation kills. This completes the integral lifting step.

The domain of this lift matters. For a global equation \(F_i\in\ker q_A\), we know \(\delta(F_i)=0\), hence \(\widetilde\delta(F_i)\in b\mathcal O\). We do not know that this integral value is zero. The construction supplies a derivative on \(A\); it does not assert an integral deformation satisfying all the global equations. Their possibly nonzero multiples of \(b\) are compatible with the torsion we are measuring.

5.6.8 Pass through the saturated square without losing a torsion class.

Let \(J_A=\ker x\). The derivation rule makes \(\widetilde\delta\) vanish on \(J_A^2\). If \(a\in J_A^{2,\mathrm{sat}}\), choose a nonzero \(c\in\mathcal O\) with \(ca\in J_A^2\). Then

\[ 0=\widetilde\delta(ca)=c\,\widetilde\delta(a) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad\widetilde\delta(a)=0. \]

Cancellation is valid because \(\mathcal O\) is a domain. In \(Q\), a nonzero \(c\) can annihilate a nonzero element, so the same reasoning applied directly to \(\delta\) would fail. Reducing the integral conclusion shows that \(\delta\) kills the saturated square. It also kills the equations \(F_i\), and therefore defines

\[ \lambda_\gamma:C_{-1}\longrightarrow Q, \qquad \lambda_\gamma([a])=\delta(a) \quad(a\in A_{-1}). \]

The integral lift certifies that this formula is well-defined. Its value depends only on the original derivative modulo \(b\), so the arbitrary choices of lifts on the additional variables cause no ambiguity.

5.6.9 Recover the cocycle from the cotangent functional.

Suppose two classes produce the same functional. Their derivatives agree on \(A_{-1}\), because every such element gives a class in \(C_{-1}\). Both derivatives vanish on every other weight. Every element of the graded algebra is a finite sum of homogeneous elements, so the derivatives agree on all of \(A\).

The model supplies a homogeneous lift \(a_{gij}\in A_{w_i-w_j}\) of each universal matrix entry: \(q_A(a_{gij})=r_{ij}(g)\). The earlier matrix-entry formula can be inverted:

\[ \gamma(g)_{ij} =\overline{\Psi(g)}^{-w_j}\delta(a_{gij}). \]

The scalar is a unit. Thus equality of the derivatives forces equality of all matrix cocycles, and the faithful normalization from §5.2 forces equality of the original cohomology classes. We have proved the injection

\[ \mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}[b]\hookrightarrow \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal O}(C_{-1},\mathcal O/b\mathcal O). \]

5.6.10 Count all torsion layers and pass to the whole group.

For \(b=\varpi^n\), the elementary-divisor description makes the size comparison explicit:

\[ \operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O} \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal O}(C_{-1},\mathcal O/\varpi^n\mathcal O) =\sum_i\min(a_i,n)\le\sum_i a_i =\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}C_{-1}. \]

For one summand \(\mathcal O/\varpi^{a_i}\mathcal O\), a homomorphism is determined by the image of \(1\). That image must be killed by \(\varpi^{a_i}\) in \(\mathcal O/\varpi^n\mathcal O\). This subgroup has length \(\min(a_i,n)\), explaining every term of the sum.

Since finite \(\mathcal O\)-modules have cardinality \(|\mathcal O/\varpi|^{\operatorname{length}}\), an injection gives the corresponding length inequality, even without asserting that our chosen encoding is \(\mathcal O\)-linear. For every nonzero \(b\in\mathcal O\), this yields

\[ \operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}[b] \le\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}C_{-1}. \]

Every continuous cocycle from the compact profinite group \(G_{\mathbb Q}\) into the discrete module \(D\) has finite image. A common nonzero denominator kills that image, so every class is torsion. The subgroups killed by \(\varpi^n\) increase to the whole group. Their cardinalities are bounded by the same finite number once \(C_{-1}\) has finite length, so this increasing sequence must stabilize. Consequently

\[ \mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}=\bigcup_{n\ge1}\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}[\varpi^n], \qquad \operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi} \le\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}C_{-1}. \]

This also establishes finiteness once the cotangent length is finite. Write \(e_{\mathcal O}=v_\varpi(\ell)\) for the ramification index and normalize by

\[ L_\ell(\psi)=\frac{\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}\mathcal S_{\ell,r,\psi}}{e_{\mathcal O}} \le\frac{\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal O}C_{-1}}{e_{\mathcal O}}. \]

This is the normalization used with \(v_\ell(\ell)=1\). In particular, a factor \(\varpi^{a_i}\) contributes \(a_i/e_{\mathcal O}\) units of \(\ell\)-adic depth.

The source's compatible-derivation construction joins the local matrix lift, the local deformation condition and the model's relative presentation. The separate cotangent argument then uses this integral lift to justify saturation and the length bound.

Modular families and their arithmetic points

6 Automorphy at deformation points.

Let \(\kappa=\overline{\mathbb Q}_\ell\), and choose a characteristic-zero specialization \(f:R\to\kappa\) satisfying the constrained deformation ring’s prescribed point conditions. The point retains the ramification, local type, polarization and marked normalization imposed on the universal representation. Its full representation \(\mathcal R_f\) includes a quadratic sign character \(\eta\); on \(G_K=\ker\eta\), for the imaginary quadratic field \(K\) in this construction, it is an ordinary three-dimensional representation \(\rho_f\).

The source distinguishes two sorts of relevant, two-sided points. In Type I, \(\rho_f\) is irreducible. In Type II, it has the prescribed outer rank-two block and middle line, with the outer block irreducible. Automorphy lifting supplies rank-three automorphy in the first case and modularity of a rank-two extension in the second.

Finiteness alone would not give the needed rigidity. A space can have one geometric point and still carry infinitesimal motion: \(\kappa[z]/(z^2)\) has just the point \(z=0\), but the map \(z\mapsto\epsilon\) into \(\kappa[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^2)\) is nonconstant. Selmer vanishing is used to control these invisible first-order directions, not merely to count points.

The automorphy input has its own argument, before Selmer vanishing enters. We now follow the route used by the final arithmetic-window theorem. It builds an ordinary automorphic locus, proves that the required points belong to it, and transfers the resulting automorphy statements to the points of our constrained deformation model.

  1. 6.1Specify the point and the conclusion
  2. 6.2Propagate the modular locus
  3. 6.3Recognize arithmetic points
  4. 6.4Descend and collect the three outputs
  5. 6.5Make the invariant algebra finite
  6. 6.6Include completed homogeneous coefficients
  7. 6.7Choose an integral grading translate
  8. 6.8Evaluate the formal series
  9. 6.9Recover the integral representation
  10. 6.10Check the ordinary flag
  11. 6.11Identify the same representation
  12. 6.12Restrict to an open subgroup
  13. 6.13Exclude the resulting permutation action
  14. 6.14Transfer rank-three automorphy
  15. 6.15Transfer rank-two modularity
  16. 6.16Return to rigidity and the length bound

6.1 Specify what has to be automorphic.

Keep the good arithmetic data from the cohomology construction: an odd prime \(\ell\), the quadratic CM field \(K\), its sign character \(\eta\), a positive integer \(e\), and the finite character \(\psi\). Here \(e\) is the twist exponent denoted \(r\) in §5.1; put \(\Psi=\varepsilon_\ell^e\psi\). The coefficient DVR \(\mathcal O\), prescribed local conditions, polarization and character-separation hypotheses remain part of the problem. The interface used first concerns unramified \(\mathcal O/\mathbb Z_\ell\); the later one-character estimate reduces general coefficients to this case using the prime-to-\(\ell\) character order and coefficient comparison.

Let \(R\) be the complete constrained ring and \(\mathcal R_R\) its universal polarized representation. Write \(M_R(g)\) for its matrix part. The point theorem starts on the entry algebra, generated by these matrix coefficients:

\[ B=\mathcal O[\,M_R(g)_{ab}:g\in G_{\mathbb Q},\ 0\le a,b\le2\,] \subseteq R, \qquad \xi:B\longrightarrow\kappa=\overline{\mathbb Q}_\ell. \]

The specialized entries define a polarized representation \(\mathcal R_\xi\); its restriction to \(G_K=\ker\eta\) is \(\rho_\xi\). An algebra map on \(B\) need not initially extend to the complete ring or give an integral continuous model. Constructing such a model is one of the steps below.

For Type I, the target is a regular algebraic, conjugate-self-dual cuspidal automorphic representation \(\pi\) attached to the correctly normalized representation:

\[ \rho_\xi\otimes\varepsilon_\ell^{-1} \quad\text{is attached to}\quad \pi\text{ on }\mathrm{GL}_3(\mathbb A_K). \]

Attachment includes agreement of the prescribed Frobenius characteristic polynomials with the automorphic Hecke data. The strengthened source conclusion also retains local compatibility witnesses used later. Type II has a different target: a rank-two extension \(W\) of the outer block, twisted by \(\varepsilon_\ell^{-e}\), must come from a newform of weight \(2e+1\). We will keep these conclusions separate.

One marking \(g_1\) is selected before the local object, the constrained ring and its point. Schematically, the quantifiers are

\[ \exists g_1\quad\forall\,\mathcal U,R,\xi: \bigl[\text{finite invariant algebra and the applicable Type I/II conclusion}\bigr]. \]

This uniformity is needed when the length argument considers many points of the same model.

6.2 Spread the modular condition from a seed across the required components.

The ordinary family lets the weights and representation vary while preserving the prescribed residual and local conditions. A known automorphic point gives a starting place, but it does not immediately control other branches of that family. The argument has two levels: a local theorem transfers the modular condition between branches through a suitable point; a global connectedness argument then ensures that no relevant component remains outside the modular locus. Throughout this step, “modular” means that the modular ideal vanishes. The passage to a classical automorphic representation comes in the next subsection.

  1. 6.2.1Keep track of the modular equations
  2. 6.2.2Identify the difficult direction
  3. 6.2.3Give the seed enough dimension
  4. 6.2.4Choose a suitable special-fibre curve
  5. 6.2.5Cross the local component chain
  6. 6.2.6Transfer the result through flat charts
  7. 6.2.7Connect the rank-two core
  8. 6.2.8Use rank two to control rank three
  9. 6.2.9Pass through exceptional branches
  10. 6.2.10Connect the rank-three core and prove density
  11. 6.2.11Cover the core by relevant components
  12. 6.2.12Exclude a nonmodular component
  13. 6.2.13Return to the specified arithmetic point

6.2.1 Keep track of what vanishes, and where.

Work over the larger CM field used by the construction, with linked ordinary deformation problems in ranks \(n=2,3\). Assume the prescribed arithmetic frame and its largeness conditions, together with the modular-family input package. All chart and dimension statements below concern this constrained setup. Let \(D_n\) be the universal ordinary determinant ring and \(J_n\) its modular ideal. The comparison with representation coordinates factors through a coefficient algebra \(B_n\):

\[ D_n\xrightarrow{\ \Phi_n\ }B_n\hookrightarrow A_n, \qquad M_n=\Phi_n(J_n)B_n, \qquad \alpha_n:D_n\longrightarrow A_n. \]

Here \(\Phi_n\) is the surjective comparison supplied in the setup. On \(X_n=\operatorname{Spec}A_n\), define the closed modular locus \(G_n\) by

\[ G_n=V(M_nA_n)=V(J_nA_n),\qquad \mathfrak p\in G_n \Longleftrightarrow M_n\subseteq\mathfrak p\cap B_n \Longleftrightarrow J_n\subseteq\alpha_n^{-1}(\mathfrak p). \]

Write \(U_n\) for the specified open Type I locus: the algebraic irreducibility condition used in this construction. Call an irreducible component relevant if it meets \(U_n\), and good if it is contained in \(G_n\). The target is \(U_n\subseteq G_n\). This formulation does not assert that every point of the ordinary family is a classical cusp form, or that every reducible component is modular.

6.2.2 Separate specialization from the difficult reverse implication.

Suppress the rank temporarily. In a local chart \(R\), let \(M_R\) be the extended modular ideal. A prime \(P\subseteq\mathfrak p\) represents a more generic point than \(\mathfrak p\). Closedness gives the easy direction:

\[ M_R\subseteq P\subseteq\mathfrak p \quad\Longrightarrow\quad M_R\subseteq\mathfrak p. \]

The reverse direction is the arithmetic content. For example, \(\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]/(xy)\) is connected. The ideal \((x)\) vanishes on one of its two components and at their intersection \((x,y)\), but it does not vanish on the other component \((y)\):

\[ (x)\subseteq(x,y),\qquad (y)\subseteq(x,y),\qquad (x)\nsubseteq(y). \]

Thus even meeting a modular branch at a common point is insufficient. The source's local modularity theorem supplies precisely the extra implication needed at carefully chosen points. Its arithmetic input comes from automorphic modules and patching; topology alone cannot replace it.

6.2.3 Turn the seed into a sufficiently large modular branch.

Let \(d\) be the positive integer attached to the arithmetic frame. The seed construction supplies primes \(\xi\subset A_n\) and \(Z\subset B_n\), with \(\xi\in U_n\), such that

\[ M_n\subseteq Z\subseteq\xi\cap B_n, \qquad \dim(B_n/Z)=1+nd. \]

This is stronger than merely exhibiting a modular point. It places that point over a modular coefficient branch of the expected dimension. In the chosen local chart, the seed-point comparison produces a good Type I prime \(I\) with

\[ \dim(R/I)=\dim(B_n/Z)+1=nd+2 \ \geq\ D_n^{\mathrm{prop}},\qquad D_n^{\mathrm{prop}}:=nd+1. \]

The extra dimension here belongs to this chart comparison; it is not an increase in the dimension of the automorphic weight space. The threshold \(D_n^{\mathrm{prop}}\) is the amount of room needed to find useful specialization points and to propagate between local branches. The seed itself is an input from the modular-family construction.

6.2.4 Use the dimension budget to find a testing curve.

A useful prime \(\mathfrak p\) in a chart is required to satisfy

\[ \ell\in\mathfrak p,\qquad \dim(R/\mathfrak p)=1, \qquad \rho_{\mathfrak p}\text{ is off the two triangular loci}, \qquad \psi_{v,i}\not\equiv\psi_{v,j}\pmod{\mathfrak p} (i\ne j). \]

The last comparison is on the prescribed local subgroups. It says that the ordered ordinary characters remain distinguishable there. The dimension condition describes a one-dimensional quotient in the special fibre; it does not say that \(\mathfrak p\) has height one in \(R\). Such a prime gives a curve along which the pointwise arithmetic theorem can be applied.

The dimension estimates bound the forbidden triangular and character-coincidence loci. If their defining ideals are \(\mathfrak a_1,\ldots,\mathfrak a_t\), the prime-selection lemma has the form

\[ \begin{gathered} \dim(R/I)\geq nd+1,\qquad \dim(R/\mathfrak a_j)<nd\quad(1\leq j\leq t)\\ \Longrightarrow\quad \exists\mathfrak p\supseteq I:\quad \varpi\in\mathfrak p,\quad\dim(R/\mathfrak p)=1, \quad\mathfrak a_j\nsubseteq\mathfrak p\ \text{for every }j. \end{gathered} \]

Here \(\varpi\) is a coefficient uniformizer, so \(\varpi\in\mathfrak p\) also forces \(\ell\in\mathfrak p\). The proof first finds a prime above \(I\) in the special fibre with at least \(nd\) dimensions remaining. No forbidden ideal can be contained in it, since that would contradict the strict dimension bound. Choose one element from each forbidden ideal that remains nonzero, and retain their product while enlarging the prime until its quotient has dimension one. This is prime avoidance with a dimension budget.

For the modular locus, the needed coincidence bounds use the finite-over-weight input. After transfer to \(B_n\), the exact condition used is

\[ \mathfrak m_{B_n}\subseteq\sqrt{M_n+\mathfrak l_n}. \]

The ideal \(\mathfrak l_n\) imposes residue characteristic and the specified trivial weight conditions on ordinary inertia characters. This radical containment says that, after imposing those weight conditions on the modular support, the coefficient point is forced into the distinguished residual fibre. Together with the local dimension estimates, it keeps the forbidden loci too small to absorb the whole intersection.

6.2.5 Cross one local intersection, then repeat.

In rank two, the chart has a power-series presentation with the following variable-minus-relation count:

\[ R\simeq\mathcal O'[[X_1,\ldots,X_N]]/(f_1,\ldots,f_r), \qquad N-r=2d+1. \]

The supplied commutative-algebra connectedness theorem gives local components of dimension at least \(2d+2\), and chains of minimal primes whose consecutive intersections satisfy

\[ P_0,\ldots,P_s,\qquad \dim R/(P_i+P_{i+1})\geq 2d+1. \]

The sum appears because \(V(P_i)\cap V(P_{i+1})=V(P_i+P_{i+1})\). This is stronger than merely requiring adjacent components to meet. The same chart estimates show that all its minimal components are Type I: a component of dimension at least \(2d+2\) cannot be trapped in the remaining triangular locus of dimension less than \(2d\).

Suppose \(P_i\) is good. Choose a prime \(I_i\supseteq P_i+P_{i+1}\) with \(\dim(R/I_i)\geq2d+1\), then choose a suitable \(\mathfrak p\supseteq I_i\) as in the preceding step. The transfer is

\[ \begin{aligned} M_R\subseteq P_i\subseteq I_i\subseteq\mathfrak p &\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathfrak p\text{ is modular},\\ P_{i+1}\subseteq\mathfrak p &\quad\Longrightarrow\quad M_R\subseteq P_{i+1} \quad\text{by local modularity.} \end{aligned} \]

At a Type I testing prime, the local theorem sends modularity down to every prime below it. An exceptional testing prime instead uses the rank-three rule described below. The same procedure starts the induction: from the large good seed prime \(I\), choose a testing prime and descend to a minimal prime \(P_0\subseteq I\). Induction along the finite chain then proves goodness of every required local Type I component.

To see exactly where the arithmetic theorem enters, write \(\beta:D_n\to R\) for the chart map. A suitable Type I curve prime is realized by a local map \(j:R\to k'[[t]]\) with \(\ker j=\mathfrak p\). The comparison theorems verify the required ordinary-character separation, irreducibility on the specified subgroup and density of separable characteristic polynomials. A further supplied condition rules out the specified cyclotomic-twisted intertwiners. The local modularity theorem then applies to the prime \(\beta^{-1}(P_{i+1})\):

\[ J_n\subseteq\ker(j\beta),\qquad \beta^{-1}(P_{i+1})\subseteq\ker(j\beta) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad J_n\subseteq\beta^{-1}(P_{i+1}). \]

Its arithmetic proof constructs a patched ring and an automorphic module, using auxiliary primes and dual cohomology bounds. That module witnesses modularity. A support theorem, with local Galois and cohomological control, forces its support to persist along the eligible branches; an annihilator comparison translates this back into the displayed ideal containment. Finite freeness over the weight algebra is part of that proof, but does not alone imply full support on every deformation component. The dimension estimates locate points where the theorem applies; they do not prove modularity by themselves.

6.2.6 Transfer local component propagation back to the family.

At an eligible closed point \(x\), a flat local chart gives \(f:\operatorname{Spec}R\to X_n\). Its component comparison lifts each global component \(Y\) through \(x\) to a minimal prime \(P\) of \(R\), with

\[ \overline{\{f(P)\}}=Y,\qquad U_R=f^{-1}(U_n),\qquad G_R=f^{-1}(G_n). \]

If \(Y\) meets the open set \(U_n\), its generic point \(f(P)\) also lies in \(U_n\): an open neighborhood of a specialization must contain its generalizations. If \(Y\) is good, \(P\) is therefore a good Type I starting point. Local propagation makes the prime \(P'\) corresponding to a second relevant component \(Y'\) good. Closedness now gives the desired whole-component conclusion:

\[ f(P')\in G_n \quad\Longrightarrow\quad Y'=\overline{\{f(P')\}}\subseteq G_n. \]

Flatness and the chart comparison are what justify lifting the global branches. The openness of Type I and closedness of modular support serve different purposes in this deduction.

6.2.7 Find the connected core in rank two.

The global argument uses a distinguished residual core, rather than assuming the entire ordinary family is connected. Write \(\mathfrak t_+\) for the ideal generated by the lower off-diagonal matrix entries. The core is

\[ C_n=\{\mathfrak p\in X_n: \mathfrak m_{B_n}\subseteq\mathfrak p\cap B_n, \quad\mathfrak t_+\nsubseteq\mathfrak p\}. \]

In rank two, its field-valued points can be expressed, using the supplied coordinate comparison, by lower-triangular representations with a nonzero extension coordinate \(a\):

\[ \rho_a(g)=\begin{pmatrix}\Psi(g)&0\\a(g)&\Psi(g)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad a(gh)=\Psi(h)a(g)+\Psi(g)^{-1}a(h). \]

The cocycle equation follows directly by multiplying these matrices. The required polarization, local conditions and local constancy also cut out a linear space of such \(a\). Fix a nonzero allowed coordinate \(b\). After extending the residue field to accommodate \(a\), form

\[ a_T=(1-T)a+Tb,\qquad a_0=a,\qquad a_1=b. \]

This polynomial family gives a prime \(\eta\), the kernel of its coordinate map to \(K[T]\), which specializes to both endpoint primes:

\[ \eta\subseteq\mathfrak p_a,\mathfrak p_b, \qquad \{\eta,\mathfrak p_a,\mathfrak p_b\}\subseteq C_2. \]

These three points form a connected subset in the Zariski topology because the endpoints lie in the closure of \(\eta\). Every point is joined this way to the same \(\mathfrak p_b\), proving connectedness of the core. The proof does not require every specialization of the affine line to remain in the core: some intermediate \(a_T\) may vanish. It only needs the common generic point and the two endpoints to remain there.

6.2.8 Use rank two to control the exceptional rank-three locus.

In rank three, let \(\mathfrak e\) be the ideal generated by matrix entries of odd weight difference, and set \(E_3=V(\mathfrak e)\). This is the closed exceptional locus. A Type E point is a point of \(E_3\) that also lies off both triangular loci. These are different conditions: an exceptional core point may itself be triangular. Type I in rank two means off both triangular loci; in rank three it additionally means outside \(E_3\). With the ordered weights \((1,0,-1)\), the representation modulo \(\mathfrak e\) has the form

\[ \rho(g)\bmod\mathfrak e= \begin{pmatrix} *&0&*\\0&*&0\\*&0&*\end{pmatrix} \simeq\rho_{\mathrm{corner}}(g)\oplus\chi(g). \]

The rank-two result controls the coefficient support of this outer corner. The nonsplit modified representations used in the next step are additional data, not the displayed block decomposition itself.

Concretely, once rank-two Type I points are good, the source constructs an auxiliary ideal \(K_3\subset B_3\) such that

\[ \mathfrak p\text{ Type E}\ \Longrightarrow\ K_3\subseteq\mathfrak p\cap B_3, \qquad \mathfrak m_{B_3}\subseteq\sqrt{K_3+\mathfrak l_3}. \]

This is a finite-weight support bound on the Type E locus. It is not the assertion \(J_3\subseteq\alpha_3^{-1}(\mathfrak p)\) at every exceptional point. The bound supplies the same kind of prime-selection control on a large Type E intersection that \(M_3\) supplies on a modular intersection. This is why rank two must be completed before the rank-three propagation argument.

6.2.9 Carry the modular branch through an exceptional chain.

In rank three, the local component graph uses primes minimal over a specified ideal \(I^\sharp\). For vertices \(Q,Q'\in\operatorname{Min}(I^\sharp)\), its dimension and connectedness results give

\[ Q\in\operatorname{Min}(I^\sharp) \ \Longrightarrow\ \dim(R/Q)\geq3d+2, \qquad Q\sim Q'\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \dim R/(Q+Q')\geq3d+1. \]

Every required ambient Type I minimal prime belongs to this graph. Some other vertices are exceptional thick branches, with a specified nonsplit deformation witness. Consequently one cannot simply label every vertex “Type I and modular” and repeat the rank-two induction.

Instead, an exceptional vertex carries the memory of an incoming good Type I branch. If two suitable exceptional primes lie over the same thick vertex \(Q\), they are linked:

\[ Q\subseteq\mathfrak p,\mathfrak q,\qquad \mathfrak p,\mathfrak q\text{ suitable Type E primes} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathfrak p\leftrightarrow_Q\mathfrak q. \]

The second arithmetic propagation rule applies along a finite chain of these links:

\[ \begin{gathered} P_0\subseteq\mathfrak p_0,\quad P_0\text{ Type I},\quad M_R\subseteq P_0,\\ \mathfrak p_0\leftrightarrow\mathfrak p_1 \leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\mathfrak p_s, \quad P'\subseteq\mathfrak p_s,\quad P'\text{ Type I}\\ \Longrightarrow\quad M_R\subseteq P'. \end{gathered} \]

At each large intersection, prime selection supplies another suitable exceptional point, so the remembered chain can be extended. On reaching a Type I vertex, the rule makes that outgoing branch modular. Thus the induction preserves either a good Type I vertex or an exceptional vertex equipped with a route back to one. It never needs to declare the intervening reducible representations cuspidal.

6.2.10 Connect the rank-three core and approach its exceptional points.

The rank-three core has adapted lower-triangular coordinates \((y,a)\). Their compatibility includes a quadratic term in \(y\). The supplied coordinate lemmas therefore use the transformation \((y,a)\mapsto(ty,t^2a+b)\), with \(b\) in the allowed linear cocycle space. Fixing a nonzero \(b\), the family

\[ (y_T,a_T)=\bigl(Ty,\ T^2a+(1-T)b\bigr) \]

joins an arbitrary core point at \(T=1\) to the common point \((0,b)\) at \(T=0\). As in rank two, a common generic prime proves Zariski connectedness. The square \(T^2\) is essential: it preserves the quadratic compatibility when \(y\) is multiplied by \(T\).

A second family explains density away from the exceptional locus. In these coordinates an exceptional core point has \(y=0\). Choose an allowed pair \((y_0,a_0)\) with \(y_0\ne0\). Then

\[ (y_T,a_T)=\bigl(Ty_0,\ T^2a_0+a\bigr) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \left.(y_T,a_T)\right|_{T=0}=(0,a),\qquad C_3\subseteq\overline{C_3\setminus E_3}. \]

Generically \(Ty_0\ne0\), so that generic point is outside \(E_3\), while its closure contains the chosen exceptional point. The coordinate realization and existence of the required nonzero cocycles are substantive inputs. The local Jacobson statements then refine this density to density of eligible closed core points outside \(E_3\).

6.2.11 Show that relevant components cover the core.

Let \(\mathcal C_n\) be the eligible closed points of \(C_n\), and put \(E_2=\varnothing\). The geometric inputs now give three precise facts: every relevant component meets \(C_n\); every point of \(\mathcal C_n\setminus E_n\) lies on a relevant component; and

\[ C_n\subseteq\overline{\mathcal C_n\setminus E_n}. \]

Since \(X_n\) is Noetherian, it has finitely many irreducible components. Divide the relevant ones into the good components and those not yet wholly modular, and form their unions:

\[ Y_{\mathrm{good}}=\bigcup_{Y\subseteq G_n}Y, \qquad Y_{\mathrm{bad}}=\bigcup_{Y\nsubseteq G_n}Y. \]

Both unions are closed because they are finite. Every eligible nonexceptional closed core point lies in their union; taking closures therefore proves

\[ C_n\subseteq Y_{\mathrm{good}}\cup Y_{\mathrm{bad}}. \]

This density step matters: an exceptional core point need not itself be Type I. The proof covers it by closure, rather than silently assuming every point of the core is irreducible.

6.2.12 Use connectedness to rule out a bad component.

The seed produces a good relevant component, so \(C_n\cap Y_{\mathrm{good}}\ne\varnothing\). Suppose a bad relevant component exists. Because it also meets the core, \(C_n\cap Y_{\mathrm{bad}}\ne\varnothing\). A connected space cannot be covered by two disjoint, nonempty relatively closed sets. Hence

\[ C_n\cap Y_{\mathrm{good}}\cap Y_{\mathrm{bad}}\ne\varnothing. \]

The closed-point selection theorem gives an eligible closed core point \(z\) in this intersection. Choose a good relevant component \(Y\) and a bad relevant component \(Y'\) through \(z\). The local result, transferred through the flat chart, says

\[ z\in Y\cap Y',\quad Y\subseteq G_n \quad\Longrightarrow\quad Y'\subseteq G_n, \]

contradicting the definition of \(Y'\). Thus every relevant component is good, and every Type I point lies on one of them. In rank three, \(z\) is allowed to be exceptional: that is exactly why the thick-branch transfer was needed. Connectedness identifies an intersection to cross; the arithmetic local theorem makes crossing it possible.

6.2.13 Apply the result to the original arithmetic point.

The outcome, first in rank two and then in rank three, is

\[ U_n\subseteq G_n,\qquad \mathfrak p\in U_n\ \Longrightarrow\ J_n\subseteq\alpha_n^{-1}(\mathfrak p). \]

For a field-valued Type I point \(x:D_n\to K\) realized by these coordinates, this means \(x(J_n)=0\). For the integral lift used later, an injective realization \(A\hookrightarrow K\) then gives exact annihilation already in \(A\). The next subsection adds the required arithmetic weights and local hypotheses and invokes classicality to obtain the desired automorphic representation.

\[ \begin{aligned} &\text{modular seed and dimension bounds}\\ &\quad\Longrightarrow\text{good local component}\\ &\quad\xRightarrow{\text{local arithmetic transfer and connected core}} U_n\subseteq G_n\\ &\quad\xRightarrow{\text{arithmetic-point classicality}} \text{automorphy of the specified point}. \end{aligned} \]

Scope of this expansion. The local induction, the two connected-core constructions, the exceptional transfer mechanism and the global contradiction are now separated and explained. The ordinary modular-family construction, its automorphic seeds, the arithmetic patching theorems behind the local transfer rules, and the supplying coordinate and dimension results remain inputs. Classicality is also a separate theorem. The statement is about the components controlled by this setup; it supplies no propagation between unrelated disconnected families.

Three source checkpoints: the local component-chain induction; the global connectedness contradiction; the rank-three core and density argument.

6.3 Turn a point on that locus into an automorphic representation.

Take an ordinary integral lift \(\rho\) over a complete DVR \(A\) with finite residue field and a local coefficient map \(\mathcal O\to A\), satisfying the prescribed residual, normalization and continuity conditions. Assume that an injective field realization \(j:A\hookrightarrow\kappa\) makes \(j\rho\) irreducible. Its universal specialization \(\zeta:A_n\to A\) gives

\[ \mathfrak p=\ker(j\circ\zeta),\qquad f=\zeta\circ\alpha_n:D_n\longrightarrow A. \]

Irreducibility places \(\mathfrak p\) in the locus covered by the preceding step. Thus, for every \(a\in J_n\), the modular-support inclusion gives \(j(f(a))=0\). Injectivity of \(j\) gives the stronger integral conclusion

\[ f(J_n)=0,\qquad \overline f:D_n/J_n\longrightarrow A, \qquad x=j\circ f:D_n\longrightarrow\kappa. \]

This is exact annihilation of the modular ideal in the integral model. The universal comparison also identifies its determinant and ordinary characters with those of the specified twist of \(\rho\). The construction then checks that its weights are arithmetic and regular, so the arithmetic-point classicality theorem applies to this particular irreducible representation. This pointwise statement does not require \(A\) to be finite over \(\mathcal O\); the later application chooses a finite coefficient extension.

Write \(\theta\) for the auxiliary algebraic character used in the linked construction and \(w_{n,i}\) for its ordered rank-\(n\) weights. At an embedding \(v\), the resulting arithmetic weights have the form

\[ h_{v,i}=\operatorname{wt}_{\theta,v}-e\,w_{n,i}, \qquad i<j\ \Longrightarrow\ w_{n,i}>w_{n,j} \ \Longrightarrow\ h_{v,i}<h_{v,j}. \]

The strict inequality uses \(e>0\). It is the regularity check needed by the theorem, alongside the verified local characters and determinant identity. In this setting classicality gives a regular algebraic cuspidal form with the determinant prescribed by \(x\). The supplied attachment theorem identifies it with the desired representation.

\[ x(J_n)=0\ +\ \text{irreducibility, the required arithmetic weights and local conditions} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \text{automorphic attachment at }x. \]

The construction initially supplies its strengthened local witness away from one distinguished auxiliary place \(u\). It runs again, for each rank, with a different place \(u'\). For any eligible place \(v\),

\[ u\ne u'\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(v\ne u)\ \text{or}\ (v\ne u'). \]

Thus the second construction covers the place omitted by the first. A further uniform-window theorem combines the witnesses with one final parameter. The elementary covering observation explains why two constructions are used; the uniformity and classicality theorems remain genuine inputs.

6.4 Descend to the required field and retain three outputs.

The linked construction produces automorphy over a larger abelian CM extension \(L\) containing \(K\). For rank three, the source constructs the embedding \(K\hookrightarrow L\), checks the polarization, continuity, inertia and flag conditions, and applies the stated solvable automorphic descent theorem. The normalized representation has polarization multiplier \(\varepsilon_\ell^{-2}\). Its restriction must stay irreducible over \(L\); this explains the stronger open-subgroup irreducibility test proved below.

\[ L/K\text{ abelian},\qquad (\rho\otimes\varepsilon_\ell^{-1})|_{G_L}\text{ automorphic} \quad\xRightarrow{\ \text{verified descent hypotheses}\ }\quad \rho\otimes\varepsilon_\ell^{-1}\text{ automorphic over }K. \]

The rank-two branch uses its own descent and newform theorem. Together, the constructed data provide three outputs at one marking: a special-fibre rigidity statement, rank-three automorphy for eligible integral ordinary points, and rank-two modularity for the eligible corners. The first output says that a fixed finite-index subgroup \(V\subseteq G_{\mathbb Q}\) satisfies

\[ \det(T-r_s(g))=\det(T-r_0(g)) \quad(g\in V\cap G_K) \]

for every prescribed ordinary representation \(r_s\) over a special-fibre test DVR. Such a test ring is complete, has finite residue field and kills \(\mathfrak m_{\mathcal O}\); it need not be finite over \(\mathcal O\). Here \(r_0\) is the split reference representation. This characteristic-polynomial assertion is the input from which finiteness of the invariant algebra is deduced next.

6.5 Use special-fibre curves to make the invariant algebra finite.

Choose the source's finite weighted presentation

\[ p:\mathcal O[[Z_1,\ldots,Z_m]]\twoheadrightarrow R, \qquad z_i=p(Z_i)\in\mathfrak m_R, \qquad\deg Z_i=q_i\in\mathbb Z. \]

The variables are chosen from matrix entries minus their split reference values, and the defining ideal is homogeneous. Write \(R^{(0)}\) for the completed weight-zero subring, and \(B_0\) for the entry algebra's invariant subalgebra. A nonconstant weight-zero monomial either contains a weight-zero variable or contains variables of both positive and negative weight.

Every local map \(R\to A_s\) to an allowed special-fibre test DVR gives an ordinary point through the imposed local deformation ideal and its ordinary-model theorem. The polynomial rigidity input therefore applies. Combined with the marked rank-three shape argument, it forces an upper or lower triangular representation with the prescribed diagonal. In the chosen presentation this gives

\[ z_i\longmapsto0\quad(q_i=0), \qquad \bigl[z_i\longmapsto0\text{ for every }q_i<0\bigr] \ \text{or}\ \bigl[z_i\longmapsto0\text{ for every }q_i>0\bigr]. \]

Consequently every nonconstant invariant monomial vanishes on these test curves. The source obtains the triangular shape using the marking and residual restrictions; equality of characteristic polynomials alone would not establish it.

Let \(\varpi\) be a uniformizer of \(\mathcal O\). If an invariant monomial \(u\) were outside \(\sqrt{\varpi R}\), a prime of the special fibre would avoid it. The curve-selection and DVR-embedding results used by the proof produce a test curve on which \(u\) remains nonzero, a contradiction. Contracting to weight zero and using finite generation gives

\[ \varpi R\cap R^{(0)}=\varpi R^{(0)},\qquad \mathfrak m_{R^{(0)}}\subseteq\sqrt{\varpi R^{(0)}},\qquad \mathfrak m_{R^{(0)}}^N\subseteq\varpi R^{(0)} \]

for some \(N\). The finite residue field makes \(R^{(0)}/\varpi R^{(0)}\) finite. Completeness then supplies finite generation over \(\mathcal O\), and its submodule \(B_0\) is finite as well. Intuitively, any surviving invariant direction would have been visible on a special-fibre curve; the triangularity test eliminates it. This establishes finiteness of the invariant algebra, without asserting that all of \(R\) is finite.

6.6 Bring completed homogeneous coefficients into the entry algebra.

A formal series can contain infinitely many terms, whereas \(B\) is generated by finite algebraic operations on matrix entries. Before evaluating a homogeneous formal expression with \(\xi\), the proof establishes that it actually belongs to \(B\).

For a weight-zero formal series \(F\), truncation gives \(b_n\in B_0\) with \(p(F)-b_n\in\mathfrak m_R^n\). Since \(B_0\) is finite over \(\mathcal O\), it has a continuous additive parametrization \(\phi:\mathbb Z_\ell^d\twoheadrightarrow B_0\). Consider

\[ E_n=\{v\in\mathbb Z_\ell^d:p(F)-\phi(v)\in\mathfrak m_R^n\}. \]

These sets are nonempty, closed and nested. Compactness gives \(v\in\bigcap_nE_n\); separatedness gives \(p(F)=\phi(v)\in B_0\). Thus the completed zero-weight coefficients are already in the finite entry-invariant algebra.

The finite spanning theorem for a fixed weight expresses each homogeneous series as finitely many monomials times weight-zero series. Hence every homogeneous part lies in \(B\), and so does the algebra \(R_{\mathrm{gr}}\) of finite sums of homogeneous parts. The map \(\xi\) now acts on this graded algebra. An arbitrary element of the complete ring may involve infinitely many different weights; its evaluation still needs the convergence argument below.

6.7 Find one grading translate whose coordinates are all small.

Put \(a_i=\xi(z_i)\). First consider a nonconstant invariant monomial \(u=\prod_i a_i^{n_i}\ne0\), with \(\sum_i n_iq_i=0\). Its inverse cannot be integral over \(\mathcal O\). Indeed, if a monic polynomial \(P\) of degree \(d\) killed \(u^{-1}\), its reversed polynomial would satisfy

\[ P^*(u)=u^dP(u^{-1})=0,\qquad P^*(0)=1. \]

The corresponding expression \(P^*(Z^n)\) is a weight-zero unit in the formal power-series ring, with a weight-zero inverse. Both lie in the part on which \(\xi\) is defined. A ring homomorphism cannot send their product \(1\) to zero. Therefore, for the extended \(\ell\)-adic valuation \(v\), normalized by \(v(\varpi)=1\),

\[ \prod_i a_i^{n_i}\ne0,\quad \sum_i n_iq_i=0,\quad \sum_i n_i>0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \sum_i n_iv(a_i)>0. \]

We seek a single \(t\in\kappa^\times\) making \(y_i=t^{q_i}a_i\) topologically nilpotent. Ignore zero coordinates, which already satisfy this requirement, and write \(s=v(t)\). The inequalities become

\[ s>-\frac{v(a_i)}{q_i}\quad(q_i>0),\qquad s<-\frac{v(a_j)}{q_j}\quad(q_j<0),\qquad v(a_i)>0\quad(q_i=0). \]

For a positive weight \(q_i\) and a negative weight \(q_j\), the invariant monomial \(a_i^{-q_j}a_j^{q_i}\) gives

\[ -q_jv(a_i)+q_iv(a_j)>0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad -\frac{v(a_i)}{q_i}<-\frac{v(a_j)}{q_j}. \]

Every lower bound is below every upper bound. There are finitely many coordinates, so choose a rational \(s\) between them and an algebraic \(t\) with that valuation. If only one sign occurs, choose \(s\) beyond the one remaining bound. For weights \(+1,-1\), the entire calculation is

\[ v(ab)>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad -v(a)<v(t)<v(b),\qquad v(ta)>0,\quad v(t^{-1}b)>0. \]

The grading lets us make both directions small without changing the isomorphism class of the representation. The final \(t\) need not be congruent to one or be a unit of the eventual integral coefficient ring.

6.8 Make convergence and the defining relations compatible.

For each rescaled coordinate choose \(d_i\) with \(y_i^{d_i}/\varpi\) integral. Let \(T_0\) be finite module generators of the image of the zero-weight part. The algebra

\[ C=\mathcal O[\,T_0,y_i,y_i^{d_i}/\varpi\ (1\le i\le m)\,]\subseteq\kappa, \qquad I=\sqrt{\mathfrak m_{\mathcal O}C} \]

is finite over \(\mathcal O\), because its finitely many generators are integral. The identities \(y_i^{d_i}=\varpi(y_i^{d_i}/\varpi)\) put every \(y_i\) in \(I\). Finite-module completeness and the comparison of powers of \(I\) with \(\mathfrak m_{\mathcal O}C\) make evaluation of formal series well defined.

\[ g:\mathcal O[[Z_1,\ldots,Z_m]]\longrightarrow C,\qquad Z_i\longmapsto y_i. \]

The critical compatibility is, for a homogeneous series \(F\) of weight \(j\),

\[ g(F)=t^j\xi(p(F))\quad\text{inside }\kappa. \]

For infinite series, agreement on variables does not by itself establish this identity. The proof uses finite spanning over weight zero, successive approximation modulo \(I^n\), and separatedness. If \(F\in\ker p\), homogeneity puts each of its weight components in the kernel too. Their evaluations vanish; finite truncations and \(\bigcap_n I^n=0\) then give \(g(F)=0\). Evaluation factors through the constrained ring:

\[ \Xi:R\longrightarrow C\hookrightarrow\kappa, \qquad \Xi|_{R_{\mathrm{gr}}}=t\cdot\xi, \qquad \Xi(R)\text{ finite over }\mathcal O. \]

This is the bridge from an abstract entry-algebra point to a specialization of the complete deformation ring.

6.9 Recover an integral, continuous representation.

The finitely many integral generators of \(\Xi(R)\) lie in some finite extension \(E/\mathbb Q_\ell\). Enlarge \(E\) to contain the coefficient field of \(\mathcal O\), and take its ring of integers \(\mathcal O'\). This is a complete DVR, finite over \(\mathcal O\), and

\[ R\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal O'\xhookrightarrow{j}\kappa, \qquad j\circ f=\Xi. \]

The specialization \(f\) may have a kernel. The faithful map is the inclusion \(j\), which lets us compare the integral representation exactly with its scalar extension.

The proof also establishes that \(f\) is local. Its image is a local quotient of \(R\); the integral extension to the local ring \(\mathcal O'\) preserves its maximal ideal. Hence

\[ f(\mathfrak m_R)\subseteq\mathfrak m_{\mathcal O'},\qquad f(\mathfrak m_R^n)\subseteq\mathfrak m_{\mathcal O'}^n. \]

Define \(\mathcal R'=f_*\mathcal R_R\). The universal residual identities give \(\mathcal R'\equiv\mathcal R_0\pmod{\mathfrak m_{\mathcal O'}}\). Continuity follows modulo every ideal power: locally constant universal entries modulo \(\mathfrak m_R^n\) remain locally constant after applying \(f\). The normalization equations and the prescribed killed inertia subgroup away from \(\ell\) also survive. These are actual checks on the model, in addition to finiteness of its coefficients.

6.10 Bring the ordinary flag down to the integral model.

The remaining condition at \(\ell\) requires an integral basis, congruent to the original basis modulo the maximal ideal, in which the representation is upper triangular on the appropriate local sign kernel and has inertia characters \((\Psi,1,\Psi^{-1})\).
